<result># Business-Registration Connector Audit (Secretary-of-State / entity registries only — UCC connectors excluded)

## Architecture pattern

**`src/porter_verify/connectors/base.py:76-93`** — `SourceConnector` is a `runtime_checkable` `Protocol` with three methods: `search(query: ConnectorQuery) -&gt; list[RawResult]`, `fetch(ref: SourceRef) -&gt; RawRecord`, `health() -&gt; SourceHealth`, plus class attrs `name`, `capabilities: set[str]`, `states: set[str]`. Capability constants: `CAP_ENTITY`, `CAP_STATUS`, `CAP_OFFICERS`, `CAP_SCREENSHOT` (base.py:18-21). Connectors return **raw, unparsed** data — normalization/scoring happens elsewhere (base.py:7-9).

`ConnectorRegistry` (base.py:96-133) holds connectors in a dict keyed by name; `select(state, capability)` iterates registration order, returns the first connector whose `capabilities` includes the requested one, whose `states` is empty (nationwide) or contains the state, and whose `health()` isn't `"unavailable"` (a health-check exception is treated as unavailable, never crashes selection).

**`factory.py:26-37`** (`build_default_registry`) registers, in order: Colorado, Connecticut, Oregon, Ohio, Florida (only if a `session_factory` is passed), then `MockVendorConnector` last as nationwide fallback. **Alabama, Georgia, Mississippi, Tennessee, Texas, and Virginia are NOT registered anywhere in factory.py** — their parser/ingest modules exist but there is no `*_connector.py` class and no factory wiring, so they cannot be selected by `ConnectorRegistry.select()` or participate in a live `/verify` call today.

**`staging_upsert.py:15-45`** (`upsert_staging_rows`) — generic idempotent batch upsert keyed on the `entity_id` primary key, using `postgresql`/`sqlite` dialect-specific `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` (falls back to per-row `session.merge` for other dialects). Every state ingest script calls this same function.

**`mock_vendor.py`** — `MockVendorConnector` (mock_vendor.py:82-135) is an in-memory fixture-backed connector (4 hardcoded records) implementing the same Protocol; `states = set()` (nationwide fallback), capabilities `{entity, status, officers}`.

## State-by-state findings

### Colorado (wired, live-verified)
- Parser: `connectors/colorado.py:122-146` (`parse_record`) — pure CSV-row parser.
- Connector: `connectors/colorado_connector.py:55-105` (`ColoradoOpenDataConnector`), name `colorado_sos_opendata`, `states={"CO"}`, caps `{entity, status}`.
- Ingest: `connectors/colorado_ingest.py:33-83` (`ingest_co_csv`), streamed CSV upsert.
- Acquisition: **bulk CSV file download**, no API, no scraping. URL constant: `CO_BULK_CSV_URL = "https://data.colorado.gov/api/views/4ykn-tg5h/rows.csv?accessType=DOWNLOAD"` (scripts/refresh_colorado.py:27). Also referenced in colorado_connector.py:31 (`CO_DATASET_URL`).
- Env var: `PORTER_CO_INGEST_LIMIT` (scripts/refresh_colorado.py:33) — dev row cap only, not a credential.
- Live-verified: yes. `docs/four_state_review.md:59` — "3,069,566 Colorado entities" loaded 2026-06-18; `data/state_refresh_manifest.json` marks CO `"status": "complete"`. Commit `998bd72 feat: add Colorado open-data connector`.
- Fields captured/stored (`db/models/staging.py:62-84`, `CoBusinessEntity`): entity_id (PK), entity_name, normalized_name, status_raw, entity_type, formation_date, principal_address, mailing_address, jurisdiction, source_record_url, agent_name, agent_address, officers (JSON, always empty — CO dataset doesn't publish officers), raw (full verbatim row).

### Connecticut (wired, live-verified)
- Parser: `connectors/connecticut.py:74-100` (`parse_ct_record`).
- Connector: `connectors/connecticut_connector.py:47-96` (`ConnecticutOpenDataConnector`), name `connecticut_sos_opendata`, `states={"CT"}`, caps `{entity, status}`.
- Ingest: `connectors/connecticut_ingest.py:36-106` (`ingest_ct_soda`) — paginates the Socrata SODA API.
- Acquisition: **live REST/JSON (CSV) API**, Socrata SODA, dataset `n7gp-d28j`. URL constant: `CT_SODA_URL = "https://data.ct.gov/resource/n7gp-d28j.csv"` (connecticut_ingest.py:26); `CT_DATASET_URL = "https://data.ct.gov/d/n7gp-d28j"` (connecticut_connector.py:25).
- Env var/API key: none referenced.
- Live-verified: yes. `docs/four_state_review.md:59` — "1,281,606 Connecticut entities"; manifest marks CT `"status": "complete"`. Part of commit `355e71a feat: complete four-state live connectors`.
- Fields (`db/models/staging.py:20-38`, `CtBusinessEntity`): same column set as CO (entity_id PK, entity_name, normalized_name, status_raw, entity_type, formation_date, principal_address, mailing_address, jurisdiction, source_record_url, agent_name/agent_address — always None since CT doesn't publish agents (connecticut.py:14), officers (always `[]`), raw.

### Oregon (wired, live-verified)
- Parser: `connectors/oregon.py:56-161` (`pivot_or_rows`) — pivots Oregon's **long-format** SODA rows (one row per name/address type, discriminated by `associated_name_type`) into one record per `registry_number`.
- Connector: `connectors/oregon_connector.py:48-97` (`OregonOpenDataConnector`), name `oregon_sos_opendata`, `states={"OR"}`, caps `{entity, status, officers}` — the only state connector here with `CAP_OFFICERS` (populated from "AUTHORIZED REPRESENTATIVE" rows).
- Ingest: `connectors/oregon_ingest.py:41-122` (`ingest_or_soda`) — streams SODA pages, groups by registry_number, pivots, upserts.
- Acquisition: **live REST/JSON (CSV) API**, Socrata SODA, dataset `tckn-sxa6`. URL constant: `OR_SODA_URL = "https://data.oregon.gov/resource/tckn-sxa6.csv"` (oregon_ingest.py:28); `OR_DATASET_URL = "https://data.oregon.gov/d/tckn-sxa6"` (oregon_connector.py:26).
- Env var/API key: none.
- Live-verified: yes. `docs/four_state_review.md:59-60` — "556,188 Oregon active entities"; manifest `"status": "complete"`, note "active entities only" (dataset does not publish inactive/dissolved entities at all — oregon_connector.py:5-6).
- Fields: same base column set (`db/models/staging.py:41-59`, `OrBusinessEntity`) plus officers actually populated (list of `{name, title, address}` dicts, oregon.py:116-130).

### Ohio (wired, connector coded, **NOT actually live** — source unreachable)
- Parser: `connectors/ohio.py:59-91` (`parse_oh_record`) — tolerant of varying header names across historical bulk-export versions.
- Connector: `connectors/ohio_connector.py:42-88` (`OhioOpenDataConnector`), name `ohio_sos_bulk`, `states={"OH"}`, caps `{entity, status}`.
- Ingest: `connectors/ohio_ingest.py:38-83` (`ingest_oh_file`) — CSV or ZIP file ingest (no network fetch in the ingest module itself).
- Acquisition: **manually-supplied official CSV/ZIP file or user-confirmed URL** (`scripts/refresh_ohio.py:21-38`, mutually-exclusive `--file`/`--url` args). No hardcoded bulk-download URL — connector docstring explicitly states "No unverified download endpoint is embedded" (refresh_ohio.py:19). Reference URL used only as an evidence link fallback: `OH_SOURCE_URL = "https://businesssearch.ohiosos.gov/"` (ohio_connector.py:20).
- Env var/API key: none.
- Live status: **explicitly not live-verified**. `docs/four_state_review.md:63-68` states Ohio remains `source_unavailable` — "the official business search, sitemap, business reports, and static publication routes all returned the Secretary of State maintenance/security page during the refresh. No paid vendor credential is configured." `data/state_refresh_manifest.json` (generated 2026-06-18) shows `"OH": {"status": "source_unavailable", "staged_entities": 0, ...}`. There is no `data/ohio*` directory on disk (unlike `data/florida/`, `data/colorado_business.csv`), confirming no real Ohio data has ever been ingested locally.
- Fields: same base column set (`db/models/staging.py:233-251`, `OhBusinessEntity`); `officers` field exists in the schema but is documented as never populated from this source.

### Florida (wired, live-verified — most recent/most mature)
- Parser: `connectors/florida.py:65-130` — **two** parsers: `parse_fl_fixed_width` (Sunbiz's official fixed-width byte-offset corporate record layout, e.g. entity_id bytes 1-12, legal_name bytes 13-204, status byte 205, agent_name bytes 545-586) and `parse_fl_record` (CSV fallback).
- Connector: `connectors/florida_connector.py:65-131` (`FloridaOpenDataConnector`), name `florida_sos_opendata`, `states={"FL"}`, caps `{entity, status}` (explicitly does **not** claim `CAP_OFFICERS` — comment at florida_connector.py:15-17 notes 0 of 11.6M rows have any officer data). Notably documents a real production perf bug/fix: SQLite couldn't use the `normalized_name` index via ORM `.startswith()`/`.like()` on an 11.6M-row table (~50s), fixed with raw `INDEXED BY` SQL (florida_connector.py:83-105), confirmed live per commit message.
- Ingest: `connectors/florida_ingest.py:113-159` (`ingest_fl_file`) — handles CSV, TXT (fixed-width), and ZIP (including Deflate64-compressed members via manual `inflate64` decompression, florida_ingest.py:49-67 — Sunbiz ZIPs use this less-common compression method).
- Acquisition: **manually-supplied official Sunbiz CSV/TXT/ZIP file or confirmed URL** (`scripts/refresh_florida.py:19-25`), same file-based pattern as Ohio/Alabama/etc. Reference/evidence URL: `FL_DATASET_URL = "https://dos.fl.gov/sunbiz/other-services/data-downloads/"` (florida_connector.py:36).
- Env var/API key: none.
- Live-verified: **yes, explicitly**, per commit `bbbe2ee feat: add Florida business-registry connector, live-verified end to end` — "New FloridaOpenDataConnector over fl_business_entities (11.6M real Sunbiz records already ingested)... Live-verified end to end: backend /verify against real FL entities... and the full frontend flow... against a real Florida entity (Chipotle Mexican Grill, Inc.)." On-disk evidence: `data/florida/cordata.zip`, `data/florida/20260628c.txt`, `data/florida/daily_2026`, `data/florida/cordata_extract` all present locally (florida_ingest.py handles exactly these file shapes).
- Fields: same base column set (`db/models/staging.py:128-146`, `FlBusinessEntity`); status codes normalized only for the two Sunbiz-documented values `A`→"Active", `I`→"Inactive" (florida_connector.py:40,46); other raw codes (`D`,`L`,`F`) passed through verbatim rather than guessed.

### Alabama, Georgia, Mississippi, Tennessee, Texas, Virginia — **parser+ingest code exists but NOT wired to the registry, NOT documented, NOT live-verified**

All six follow an identical, apparently template-generated pattern:
- Parser module (`alabama.py`, `georgia.py`, `mississippi.py`, `tennessee.py`, `texas.py`, `virginia.py`) — a frozen dataclass (`AlBusinessRecord`/`GaBusinessRecord`/etc.) and a `parse_&lt;state&gt;_record(row: dict)` function using a case-insensitive `_first(row, *names)` header-alias lookup (multiple possible CSV column-name spellings tried in order) and a small multi-format date parser. Each embeds a **reference URL builder** pointing at the state's live search portal, used only as a fallback evidence link, e.g.:
  - Alabama: `f"https://arc-sos.state.al.us/cgi/corpdetail.mbr/detail?corp={entity_id}"` (alabama.py:55)
  - Georgia: `f"https://ecorp.sos.ga.gov/BusinessSearch/BusinessInformation?businessId={entity_id}"` (georgia.py:50)
  - Mississippi: `f"https://corp.sos.ms.gov/corp/portal/c/page/corpBusinessIdSearch/portal.aspx?businessId={entity_id}"` (mississippi.py:50)
  - Tennessee: `f"https://tnbear.tn.gov/Ecommerce/FilingSearch.aspx?CN={entity_id}"` (tennessee.py:50)
  - Texas: `"https://mycpa.cpa.state.tx.us/coa/search.do"` (texas.py:50, static, not entity-specific)
  - Virginia: `f"https://cis.scc.virginia.gov/EntitySearch/BusinessInformation?businessId={entity_id}"` (virginia.py:49)
  These are **UI search-portal URLs, not data/API/bulk-download endpoints** — none of the six states has a bulk-download or SODA URL constant anywhere in their module (unlike CO/CT/OR/FL). No import of BeautifulSoup/playwright/lxml/scraping libraries exists in any of them — they are pure CSV/ZIP-row parsers.
- Ingest module (`alabama_ingest.py`, `georgia_ingest.py`, `mississippi_ingest.py`, `tennessee_ingest.py`, `texas_ingest.py`, `virginia_ingest.py`) — each is a near-verbatim copy of the same CSV/ZIP-streaming-and-upsert pattern (`_rows()` handling `.zip` via `zipfile`/`csv.DictReader`, then batched `upsert_staging_rows`), e.g. `ingest_al_file` (alabama_ingest.py:38-82), `ingest_ga_file` (georgia_ingest.py:38-82), etc.
- Refresh script for each (`scripts/refresh_alabama.py`, `refresh_georgia.py`, `refresh_mississippi.py`, `refresh_tennessee.py`, `refresh_texas.py`, `refresh_virginia.py`) — all identical boilerplate: `argparse` mutually-exclusive `--file`/`--url`, optional `httpx.stream` download to a temp file, then call the state's `ingest_*_file`. Each docstring/description says "No unverified bulk URL is embedded" — i.e., **acquisition method is manual file supply**, not an automated live connection.
- **No `*_connector.py` file exists** for any of these six states, so there is no `SourceConnector`-Protocol implementation and no entry in `factory.py`. They cannot currently serve a `/verify` request for their state via the registry.
- **Not mentioned in any of the three docs** (`four_state_review.md`, `source_registry.md`, `state_source_feasibility.md` — the "four states" the docs describe are CO/CT/OR/OH only; FL was added later by a commit but the docs were not updated to add Florida or these six).
- **No commit message claims "live-verified"** for any of them; `git log` shows only two ingest-related commits touching these six files — `767f757 feat: stabilise &amp; ship...` and `355e71a feat: complete four-state live connectors` — neither commit message names Alabama/Georgia/Mississippi/Tennessee/Texas/Virginia specifically.
- **No local data files exist** for these states under `data/` (only `data/colorado_business.csv`, `data/co_sample.csv`, `data/colorado_ucc/`, `data/florida/`, `data/florida_ucc/`, `data/ofac_sdn.csv` are present — no `data/alabama*`, `data/georgia*`, `data/mississippi*`, `data/tennessee*`, `data/texas*`, `data/virginia*`).
- DB tables (`AlBusinessEntity`, `GaBusinessEntity`, `TnBusinessEntity`, `TxBusinessEntity`, `VaBusinessEntity`, `MsBusinessEntity`) exist in `db/models/staging.py:86-231` with the identical column set as the wired states (entity_id PK, entity_name, normalized_name, status_raw, entity_type, formation_date, principal_address, mailing_address, jurisdiction, source_record_url, agent_name, agent_address, officers JSON — always `[]` since none of the six parsers populate officers, raw JSON), but nothing has ever populated them in this environment.
- Env vars/API keys: none referenced in any of the six modules or their refresh scripts.

**Conclusion for these six: scaffolded/stubbed only** — parsing logic and staging schema are built and structurally identical to the four "real" states, but there is no live acquisition path, no registry wiring, no test data, and no documentation or commit claiming verification. `state_source_feasibility.md:65-70` explicitly lists AL, GA, MS, TN, VA (not TX, which appears separately at line 52 as "✅ verified (scope check needed)" under Tier A) among the ~38 states in the **"Tier B/C — unverified, defaults to live-query/scraping, needs legal sign-off"** bucket — i.e., per the project's own planning doc, these states were *not* cleared as safe bulk/open-data sources before this parser code was written, which is inconsistent with the "no scraping without legal sign-off" guardrail stated at `source_registry.md:45-46` and `state_source_feasibility.md:85`.

## Summary

**States with a wired, registered `SourceConnector` (`factory.py:26-37`):** Colorado, Connecticut, Oregon, Ohio, Florida — 5 states.
**Live-verified with real ingested data (per docs/commits/on-disk evidence):** Colorado, Connecticut, Oregon, Florida — 4 states.
**Wired but not live (source unreachable, zero rows):** Ohio.
**Parser/ingest code exists but NOT wired into the connector registry, NOT documented, NOT live-verified, NOT legally cleared per the project's own feasibility doc:** Alabama, Georgia, Mississippi, Tennessee, Texas, Virginia — 6 states.
**States referenced only in docs with no code at all:** none of the doc-listed Tier-A candidates (Arkansas, Minnesota, California, Washington, Iowa, Illinois — `state_source_feasibility.md:49-55`) have any connector/parser/ingest files in this repo.

**`data/` directory / .gitignore:** `.gitignore:38` has a single blanket `data/` rule (not per-file entries as might be assumed) — everything under `data/` is untracked, including `data/colorado_business.csv`, `data/co_sample.csv`, `data/colorado_ucc/*`, `data/florida/*` (cordata.zip, extracted files, daily updates), `data/florida_ucc/*`, `data/ofac_sdn.csv`, `data/state_refresh_manifest.json`, plus log files (`api.log`, `frontend.log`, etc.). The manifest (`data/state_refresh_manifest.json`, generated 2026-06-18, predates the Florida connector) records exact row counts for CO/CT/OR and the Ohio failure reason, but has never been updated to include Florida or any of the six unwired states.

**Reusable base pattern:** `SourceConnector` Protocol (`base.py`) + `ConnectorRegistry` (capability+state+health selection) + `upsert_staging_rows` (`staging_upsert.py`, dialect-aware idempotent batch upsert keyed on `entity_id`) is the common backbone every state (wired or not) is built on. Adding a new state connector following this repo's own convention (`docs/source_registry.md:39-46`) means: write a parser module, a staging model, a `*_connector.py` implementing the Protocol, an ingest module reusing `upsert_staging_rows`, a `refresh_&lt;state&gt;.py` script, and a `factory.py` registration — the six unwired states have everything except the connector class and the factory registration.

**NAICS/SIC/industry-code field:** **Does not exist anywhere** in `BusinessRegistration`, `Company`, `CompanyIdentifier`, `RegisteredAgent`, `CompanyOfficer` (`db/models/registration.py`, `db/models/company.py`) or in any of the eight `*BusinessEntity` staging models (`db/models/staging.py`). The only place an industry code could survive today is inside the catch-all `raw: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(JSON)` column present on every staging table — which preserves whatever columns a given state's source file happens to include, unparsed and unindexed. No parser in any of the eleven state modules extracts or maps a NAICS/SIC field, and no `normalize_name`/status-normalization equivalent exists for industry codes.</result>

# AUDIT REPORT: porter_verify (Search Intelligence)

## A) DB Models

**Base infrastructure** — `src/porter_verify/db/base.py`
- `Base` (line 33): SQLAlchemy `DeclarativeBase` with a fixed `NAMING_CONVENTION` (line 24-30) for stable constraint names across dialects.
- `uuid_pk()` (line 45-48): UUID PK, Python-side default (`uuid.uuid4`), portable across SQLite/Postgres via SQLAlchemy `Uuid` type.
- `TimestampMixin` (line 51-59): `created_at`/`updated_at`, both `DateTime(timezone=True)`, UTC default, `updated_at` has `onupdate`.
- `CapturedAtMixin` (line 62-67): single immutable `captured_at` timestamp for append-only/WORM rows.

**Company spine** — `db/models/company.py`
- `Company` (`companies`, line 22-40): `canonical_legal_name`, `normalized_name` (indexed), `home_state` (2-char), `status_normalized` (enum `RegistrationStatus`), `entity_age_days`, `dedupe_key` (unique, `home_state|normalized_name`). Has `identifiers` relationship.
- `CompanyIdentifier` (`company_identifiers`, line 43-57): `company_id` FK, `id_type` (enum `IdentifierType`, e.g. state reg #, UEI), `id_value`, `source_id` FK → provenance. Unique on `(id_type, id_value)`.

**Registrations / agents / officers** — `db/models/registration.py`
- `BusinessRegistration` (`business_registrations`, line 19-46): `company_id` FK, `state` (2-char), `state_entity_id`, `entity_type`, `formation_date` (Date), `principal_address`, `mailing_address`, `jurisdiction`, `source_record_url`, `status_raw` + `status_normalized`, `source_id` FK. Unique `(state, state_entity_id)`.
- `RegisteredAgent` (`registered_agents`, line 49-62): `registration_id` FK, `agent_name`, `agent_address`, `source_id` FK. Uses `CapturedAtMixin` (append-only fact, PII-adjacent per docstring).
- `CompanyOfficer` (`company_officers`, line 65-78): `company_id` FK, `name`, `title`, `address`, `screened_ofac` (bool|null), `source_id` FK. PII, `CapturedAtMixin`.

**Verification runs / evidence (WORM)** — `db/models/verification.py`
- `VerificationRun` (`verification_runs`, line 31-71): `company_id`, `requested_by`, `trigger`, `match_confidence`, `risk_score`, `status` (`RunStatus` enum: PENDING/RUNNING/COMPLETED/FAILED/SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE — `db/enums.py:33-40`), `verification_status` (`VerificationStatus` enum), `score_version`, `started_at`, `finished_at`, `idempotency_key` (unique, indexed, line 62), `query_name`/`query_name_normalized`/`query_state` (line 64-67, enables `find_cached_run`). Relationships to `raw_events`, `evidence`, `scores`.
- `RawSourceEvent` (`raw_source_events`, line 74-95): verbatim pre-parse response — `request`, `response_blob`, `response_code`, `latency_ms`, `raw_hash` (SHA-256), `cost_credits`, `occurred_at`. Append-only, drives quality aggregation (see C/D).
- `EvidenceItem` (`evidence_items`, line 98-116): `storage_uri`, `sha256`, `source_url`, `type` (enum `EvidenceType`). WORM.
- `ConfidenceScore` (`confidence_scores`, line 119-133): `component`, `value`, `weight`, `explanation` — one row per scoring factor per run.
- File docstring (line 1-8) explicitly states these three tables are WORM: production DB role lacks UPDATE/DELETE; only `VerificationRun` itself is updated for its own terminal state.

**Source registry / governance / quality** — `db/models/source.py`
- `SourceRegistry` (`source_registry`, line 28-49): `name` (unique), `capabilities` (JSON list), `states` (JSON list), `cost_per_lookup`, `health_status` (string, set by `evaluate_source_health`), **`enabled`** (Boolean, default True — line 41, this is the kill switch). Relationships: `credentials`, `policy` (1:1), `quality_daily` (1:many).
- `SourceCredential` (`source_credentials`, line 52-64): `secret_ref` pointer only (e.g. `vault://...`), never plaintext; `env`, `rotated_at`.
- `SourcePolicy` (`source_policies`, line 67-86): `acquisition_method`, `legal_review_status`, `allowed_purposes` (JSON), `retention_days`, `freshness_sla_hours`, `terms_url`, `owner`, `approved_by`/`approved_at`. Added by migration `f3a1c9b7d2e4_add_source_governance.py`.
- `SourceQualityDaily` (`source_quality_daily`, line 89-106): `source_id`, `metric_date`, `request_count`, `success_count`, `record_count`, `avg_latency_ms`, `freshness_pass_rate`, `estimated_cost`, `schema_drift_detected` (bool). Unique `(source_id, metric_date)`. Also added by `f3a1c9b7d2e4`.

**Per-state staging tables** — `db/models/staging.py` (lines 20-251): 10 near-identical tables — `CtBusinessEntity`, `OrBusinessEntity`, `CoBusinessEntity`, `AlBusinessEntity`, `GaBusinessEntity`, `FlBusinessEntity`, `TnBusinessEntity`, `TxBusinessEntity`, `VaBusinessEntity`, `MsBusinessEntity`, `OhBusinessEntity` — each with `entity_id` (PK, natural state id), `entity_name`, `normalized_name` (indexed), `status_raw`, `entity_type`, `formation_date` (indexed), `principal_address`/`mailing_address` (Text), `jurisdiction`, `source_record_url`, `agent_name`/`agent_address`, `officers` (JSON list), `raw` (JSON, full verbatim row). Per file docstring (line 1-8), staging is a **cache of bulk-downloaded open-data sources**, distinct from the canonical `Company` spine — the connector queries staging, but a verify run still goes through the same evidence/audit path as a live source.

**UCC** — `db/models/ucc.py`
- `UccSearchOrder` (`ucc_search_orders`, line 15-38): human-ordered exact-name state UCC search; `status` (`UccSearchStatus`), `outcome` (`UccSearchOutcome`), `requested_by_email`/`completed_by_email`.
- `UccFiling` (`ucc_filings`, line 41-69): normalized UCC filing — `debtor_name`/`debtor_normalized`, `secured_party_name`/`normalized`, `collateral_description`, `filing_date`/`termination_date`, `lender_type`, `is_factoring_related`, `is_mca_related`, `acquisition_method`, `match_confidence`.
- `KnownFactor` (`known_factors`, line 72-83): watchlist of factoring/MCA/ABL/bank lenders.
- `UccExitSignal` (`ucc_exit_signals`, line 86-103): UCC-1→UCC-3 termination lead signal, `signal_strength`, `days_since_exit`.
- `UccLead` (`ucc_leads`, line 106-133): promoted sales lead from an exit signal, `status` (`UccLeadStatus`), unique on `exit_signal_id` (idempotent promotion).
- `UccRefreshLog` (`ucc_refresh_log`, line 136-149): append-only log of refresh attempts — `state`, `refresh_type`, `records_added/updated`, `started_at/finished_at`, `status`, `error_text`.

**Webhooks** — `db/models/webhook.py`
- `WebhookEndpoint` (`webhook_endpoints`, line 22-39): `url`, `secret` (shown once), `enabled`, `created_by_email`.
- `WebhookDelivery` (`webhook_deliveries`, line 42-73): `endpoint_id`, `verification_run_id`, `event_type`, `payload` (JSON), `status` (`WebhookDeliveryStatus`: PENDING/DELIVERED/FAILED), `attempt_count`, `last_attempt_at`, `next_retry_at`, `response_code`, `error`. Unique `(endpoint_id, verification_run_id)` (line 46-50) — idempotent, one delivery row per run per endpoint.

**Audit / errors / reports** — `db/models/audit.py`
- `AuditLog` (`audit_logs`, line 14-38): append-only, `actor`, `action`, `entity_type/id`, `before`/`after` (JSON), `request_id`, `occurred_at`. BigInteger PK (autoincrement, sqlite falls back to Integer).
- `ErrorLog` (`error_logs`, line 41-53): `source_id`, `run_id`, `error_type`, `message`.
- `GeneratedReport` (`generated_reports`, line 56-65): KYB PDF, `storage_uri`, `sha256`, `generated_by`.

**Review** — `db/models/review.py`: `ReviewDecisionRecord` (`review_decisions`, line 14-36) — immutable reviewer decision, `decision` (enum `ReviewDecision`), `candidate_chosen`, `before`/`after` snapshots.

**Salesforce** — `db/models/salesforce.py`: `SalesforceSyncStatus` (`salesforce_sync_status`, line 15-36) — `sf_object`/`sf_record_id` (unique pair), `sync_status` (enum `SyncStatus`), explicitly a "Phase 3" foundation, docstring says no data pushed until credentials/mappings/approval flow exist.

**Users** — `db/models/user.py`: `Role` (`roles`) with JSON `permissions` list; `User` (`users`) with `email` (unique), `sso_sub`, `role_id` FK, `active`.

## B) Freshness, scheduling, automation

There **is** a real in-process scheduler — `src/porter_verify/scheduler.py`, built on **APScheduler's `BackgroundScheduler`** (line 8, 42). It is not a stub.

- Wired in `src/porter_verify/api/app.py` lifespan (line 135-139): `if settings.scheduler_enabled: scheduler = build_scheduler(); scheduler.start()`. Shut down cleanly on app shutdown (line 143-146).
- `settings.scheduler_enabled` is read from `PORTER_SCHEDULER_ENABLED` via `config.py:47` (`scheduler_enabled: bool = True`, prefix `PORTER_`). Default **on**.
- Jobs registered in `build_scheduler()` (`scheduler.py:41-127`):
  - `OFAC_REFRESH_JOB_ID` — cron Sun 02:00 → `refresh_ofac_files()`
  - `UCC_WEEKLY_REFRESH_JOB_ID` — cron Sun 03:00 → `detect_exit_signals` + refresh log
  - `CT_UCC_REFRESH_JOB_ID` — cron Sun 03:15 → fetch+ingest CT UCC rows
  - `CO_UCC_REFRESH_JOB_ID` — cron Sun 03:30 → CO UCC rows
  - `OR_UCC_REFRESH_JOB_ID` — cron Sun 03:45 → OR UCC rows
  - `FL_UCC_REFRESH_JOB_ID` — cron Sun 04:00 → ingest FL UCC zip dir
  - `UCC_DAILY_INCREMENTAL_JOB_ID` — cron daily 06:00 → `detect_exit_signals` again (incremental)
  - `SOURCE_QUALITY_JOB_ID` — cron daily 01:00 → `compute_daily_source_quality` + `evaluate_source_health` (D)
  - `STUCK_RUN_SWEEP_JOB_ID` — interval every 5 min → `_sweep_stuck_runs` (C)
  - `WEBHOOK_RETRY_JOB_ID` — interval every 2 min → `retry_due_deliveries` (C)

  All jobs use `replace_existing=True`. Note: none of the state business-registry connectors (CT/OR/CO/AL/GA/FL/TN/TX/VA/MS/OH bulk business entities) appear scheduled here — **only the UCC-specific refreshes and OFAC are automated**; the general business-entity staging ingests (per `scripts/refresh_*.py`, not read in this pass but referenced in staging.py docstring) look like manual/one-off script invocations, not scheduler jobs. Confirm by checking `scripts/` directory if this distinction matters — I did not enumerate `scripts/*.py` in this pass.
- `workers/` directory contains **only** `verify_flow.py` (the per-request verification pipeline) — no generic "ingest worker" module; the actual per-source ingestion functions live in `connectors/*` (e.g. `connectors/colorado_ucc.py`, `connectors/florida_ucc.py`) and are called directly by `scheduler.py`.
- `.github/workflows/ci.yml` triggers only on `push`/`pull_request` (line 6-9) — confirmed **no `schedule:`/cron trigger**, so there is no GitHub-Actions-driven data refresh; all recurring automation is the in-process APScheduler above, which only runs while the FastAPI process is alive.
- `ucc_public_search.py:304` comment confirms "opposed to bulk-loaded states, which are ingested via scheduler.py cron" — consistent with the above.

**Bottom line for B:** Freshness/refresh automation exists and is real (APScheduler cron/interval jobs inside the running API process), gated by `PORTER_SCHEDULER_ENABLED`. It covers OFAC, 4 states' UCC filings, UCC exit-signal detection, source-quality rollup, stuck-run sweeping, and webhook retries. It does **not** run as a separate process/container and does **not** have an external (GH Actions / OS-level Task Scheduler / cron file) trigger — if the API process isn't running, nothing refreshes.

---

## C) Async jobs / idempotency / webhooks / sweeper

There's no generic "jobs" table — the job/run abstraction **is** `VerificationRun` (`db/models/verification.py:31`), and webhook delivery is tracked in its own `WebhookDelivery` table.

**Async run lifecycle** (`workers/verify_flow.py`):
- `create_pending_run` (line 117-154) — creates a `PENDING` `VerificationRun`, commits, returns immediately so the API can hand back a `run_id`.
- `run_in_background` (line 551-591) — passed to FastAPI `BackgroundTasks`; runs `execute_pending_run` in its own DB session; on any exception, marks the run `FAILED` with an `ErrorLog` in a **fresh** session (in case the original session is broken) — never leaves it silently hanging (line 581-591).
- API side: `api/routers/verify.py` `POST /verify` (line 80-153) returns HTTP 202 with `run_id`; `GET /runs/{run_id}` (`api/routers/runs.py:18-34`) is the poll endpoint.
- **Idempotency**: client-supplied `Idempotency-Key` header (line 90, 95-101) — `find_run_by_idempotency_key` (verify_flow.py:157-162) returns the original run rather than starting a duplicate/re-charging. Enforced at the DB level too: `VerificationRun.idempotency_key` is `unique=True, index=True` (verification.py:62), added by migration `d4e8f1a3c6b9_add_run_idempotency_and_webhooks`.
- **Response caching** (separate from idempotency key): `find_cached_run` (verify_flow.py:165-192) — a repeat `(name, state)` query within `PORTER_...verify_cache_ttl_minutes` (config.py:69, default 1440 = 24h) is served from the prior `COMPLETED` run, marked `cached=True` with `cache_age_seconds` in the response (verify.py:111-124). This is a TTL cache keyed on normalized name+state, not a generic cache table.

**Sweeper** (`api/app.py`):
- `_sweep_stuck_runs` (line 76-108) — marks `PENDING` runs older than 10 min (`_STUCK_RUN_CUTOFF_MINUTES`, line 53) or `RUNNING` runs older than 20 min (`_STUCK_RUNNING_CUTOFF_MINUTES`, line 54) as `FAILED`. Run once at app startup (line 134) to catch runs orphaned by a process restart, **and** every 5 minutes via the scheduler's `_sweep_stuck_runs_job` (scheduler.py:138-148) to catch a background task that died without a process restart. This is the "sweeper fix" mentioned in the git log.

**Webhooks** (`services/webhooks.py`, models in `webhook.py`):
- `enqueue_and_deliver` (line 68-96) — on every finished run, for each enabled `WebhookEndpoint`, idempotently creates (or skips if already present, line 79-86) one `WebhookDelivery` row per `(endpoint_id, run_id)` and attempts immediate delivery.
- Delivery is HMAC-SHA256 signed (`sign_payload`, line 48-51) with a per-endpoint secret shown once at creation (`generate_secret`, line 42-45), header `X-Porter-Signature`.
- `_attempt_delivery` / `_schedule_retry_or_fail` (line 119-158): exponential-ish backoff `[1, 5, 15, 60, 240]` minutes (line 37) up to `MAX_ATTEMPTS = 5` (line 35), then `WebhookDeliveryStatus.FAILED` for manual inspection via `GET /webhooks/deliveries`.
- `retry_due_deliveries` (line 99-116) is invoked every 2 minutes by the scheduler's `WEBHOOK_RETRY_JOB_ID`.
- `_dispatch_webhooks` in `verify_flow.py:106-114` wraps delivery in try/except so a webhook failure never affects the run's own recorded result — best-effort by design.

**Source-quality daily aggregation feeds C too**: raw per-call facts are captured on every source call as `RawSourceEvent` (verification.py:74-95) during `_execute` (verify_flow.py, e.g. lines 350-360, 411-422) — this append-only event log is the input the daily quality job rolls up (see D).

---

## D) Source quality scoring / kill switch

**Kill switch** — a per-source boolean, `SourceRegistry.enabled` (`db/models/source.py:41`, default `True`).
- Enforced in the verification flow itself: `workers/verify_flow.py:305-324` — "Respect the kill switch: an admin-disabled source is treated the same as 'no connector available' — no charge, no attempted call." Sets `RunStatus.SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE` / `VerificationStatus.INSUFFICIENT_EVIDENCE`, logs an `ErrorLog(error_type="source_disabled")`.
- Toggled via `PATCH /sources/{source_name}/enabled` (`api/routers/sources.py:43-76`), restricted to `admin` role (line 48), writes an `AuditLog` entry with before/after (line 65-73). Explicitly described in the docstring (line 50-58) as a **deliberate, human, audited action** — never automatic.
- Distinguished from `health_status` (a string field also on `SourceRegistry`), which **is** set automatically by quality evaluation but never touches `enabled` — see next.

**Quality scoring/aggregation** — `services/source_quality.py`:
- `compute_daily_source_quality` (line 48-110): reads `RawSourceEvent` rows for a `metric_date`, rolls up per source into `SourceQualityDaily` — `request_count`, `success_count` (2xx responses, `_SUCCESS_CODE_MIN/MAX` = 200/299, line 38-39), `record_count`, `avg_latency_ms`, `estimated_cost` (sum of `cost_credits`), `freshness_pass_rate`, `schema_drift_detected` (always `False` for now — explicitly deferred, line 17-22, "a larger effort deferred to a follow-up"). Idempotent per `(source_id, metric_date)` (upsert via unique constraint).
- `_freshness_pass_rate` (line 113-122): fraction of successful calls whose latency was within the source's `SourcePolicy.freshness_sla_hours`; returns `None` (not 0%) when no SLA is configured or nothing measurable — explicit "not measured" vs "failing" distinction.
- `evaluate_source_health` (line 125-159): reads each source's most recent `SourceQualityDaily` row, computes `success_rate = success_count/request_count`, classifies `health_status` as `"healthy"` (≥0.95, `_HEALTHY_SUCCESS_RATE`), `"degraded"` (≥0.80, `_DEGRADED_SUCCESS_RATE`), or `"unhealthy"` (below). Sources with zero requests that day are left alone ("silence isn't failure", line 43). **Explicitly does not touch `enabled`** (docstring line 128-130) — health is informational; disabling is always the admin's `PATCH .../enabled` call.
- Both are run daily by the scheduler's `_compute_source_quality_job` (scheduler.py:151-165) against **yesterday's** date (complete day of events), and exposed read-only via `GET /sources/health` and `GET /sources/quality` (`api/routers/sources.py:24-40`, `ops` role).

**Source governance** (`services/sources.py`, model `SourcePolicy`):
- `upsert_source_policy` (line 18-43) creates/updates a `SourcePolicy` row (`acquisition_method`, `legal_review_status`, `allowed_purposes`, `retention_days`, `freshness_sla_hours`, `terms_url`, `owner`, `approved_by/at`) via `PUT /sources/{source_name}/policy` (admin-only, audited).
- Added by migration `f3a1c9b7d2e4_add_source_governance.py` — creates exactly `source_policies` and `source_quality_daily`.

**"Cobalt" reference** — `docs/cobalt_analysis_and_plan.md`. This is **not** a paid vendor integration in this codebase; it's a competitive-analysis-and-build-plan document (v2.0, dated 2026-06-18) that:
- Analyzes **Cobalt Intelligence**, a real third-party business-verification/KYB SaaS product (SOS lookup, async `retryId` jobs, batch CSV, UCC, TIN matching, sanctions screening) based only on its **public** docs/site (explicit evidence-standard section, line 7-16: "Verified" vs "Inferred" vs "Unknown"; states repeatedly it never used private APIs or bypassed auth).
- Recommends Porter build its **own** system inspired by good patterns (source registry, immutable evidence, async job model, idempotency, webhooks, source-quality metrics) rather than integrate with or clone Cobalt (line 18-27: "Porter should not clone it... use the best Cobalt patterns as one input").
- `api/routers/verify.py:5-6` docstring explicitly cites this: "Live source lookups can be slow... so verification is async (**Cobalt's retryId pattern**)" — i.e., the async-run/poll design was modeled on Cobalt's publicly documented pattern, confirming "Cobalt-parity" in the git log means design-pattern parity, not an actual Cobalt API integration or credential anywhere in this repo. No Cobalt API key, base URL, or SDK call exists in `src/`.
- The doc's §4.2/§5 lay out a 10-phase future plan (signals, lead scoring, Salesforce push, contact enrichment, alerts, agents, voice outreach) — almost all of it **not yet built**; Phase 3-4 (source registry/policy + evidence pipeline) and part of Phase 8 (source quality) are what's actually implemented today per A/D above.

All three research agents are back. Here's the full audit.

## 1. What it is

**System**: `porter_verify` (FastAPI service), lives entirely in `src/porter_verify/` in this repo. Branch context: `bbbe2ee` (Florida connector), `ef75aad` (Phase 4 async jobs), etc. — this is one continuously-developed internal product ("Porter Verify"), not a separate script or third-party tool.

- **Combination of**: a FastAPI API (`api/app.py`), a Postgres/SQLite DB (SQLAlchemy + Alembic, 21 linear migrations), an in-process APScheduler (`scheduler.py`) for recurring refresh jobs, per-state Python "connector" modules that call live APIs or parse bulk files, and a set of manual CLI refresh scripts (`scripts/refresh_*.py`).
- No dedicated ops team identified in code; it's a single evolving codebase — built/maintained via the commits in this repo (git blame/author would show you who, not investigated here).
- Two unrelated things are just sitting in the working tree, untracked: **`OpenOutreach/`** is a full separate clone (its own `.git`, remote `github.com/eracle/OpenOutreach`) of a third-party Django app for LinkedIn-scraping + email outreach — zero integration with `porter_verify`. **`nj_walmart.html`** is a stray saved HTML dump of an NJ UCC search-results page from manual testing of `new_jersey_ucc.py` — not referenced anywhere, contains no "Walmart" data.

## 2. State coverage

**Business-registry (Secretary-of-State entity) connectors** — architecture is a `SourceConnector` Protocol + `ConnectorRegistry` (`connectors/base.py`), wired in `connectors/factory.py`:

| State | Wired to registry? | Live status | Method |
|---|---|---|---|
| Colorado | Yes | **Live, verified** — 3.07M entities | Bulk CSV download, hardcoded URL (data.colorado.gov) |
| Connecticut | Yes | **Live, verified** — 1.28M entities | Live REST/JSON API (Socrata SODA) |
| Oregon | Yes | **Live, verified** — 556K *active-only* entities | Live REST/JSON API (Socrata SODA); dataset never includes inactive/dissolved entities |
| Florida | Yes | **Live, verified** — 11.6M Sunbiz records | Manually-supplied official fixed-width/CSV/ZIP file (no bulk URL hardcoded) |
| Ohio | Yes (registered) | **Not live** — SOS site returns maintenance/security page; 0 rows staged | Would be manual file, but source unreachable |
| Alabama, Georgia, Mississippi, Tennessee, Texas, Virginia | **No** — parser + staging table exist, but no connector class, no factory registration | Never run; no data on disk | N/A — code is scaffolded only, and per the project's own `state_source_feasibility.md`, these states weren't legally cleared for scraping before this code was written |

**UCC connectors** — 23 state-specific files (`connectors/*_ucc.py`):
- **Bulk/live-loaded (fully automated)**: Connecticut, Colorado, Florida (all confirmed with real record counts in `docs/ucc_state_research.md`), Oregon (partial — recent-month dataset only, not full historical).
- **Washington**: file-import only, no verified bulk URL exists.
- **Targeted live public-search (Playwright/httpx, confirmed working)**: New Jersey, Idaho, New York, Michigan, South Carolina, Kentucky, Wisconsin, Arkansas, Minnesota (file-number lookup only; debtor-name search needs paid MBLS credentials).
- **Blocked/gated (code exists, raises a typed error, routes to manual review — not silent stubs)**: Arizona, California, Illinois, Pennsylvania, North Carolina (Cloudflare/Incapsula/Akamai bot walls), Maryland, Indiana (CAPTCHA walls), Nevada, Missouri (both permanently unreachable without a paid account — code unconditionally raises).
- **Automated/scheduled (cron)**: only CT, CO, OR, FL UCC + OFAC + UCC exit-signal detection run on a schedule. Everything else (all 6 unwired business-registry states, all targeted/blocked UCC connectors) is manual-trigger-only via `scripts/refresh_*.py`.

## 3. Data details

**Format**: Postgres/SQLite via SQLAlchemy models, migrated with Alembic (21 revisions). No CSV/flat-file is the system of record — raw downloaded files (Sunbiz ZIPs, CT/CO/OR CSVs) sit in an untracked `data/` directory (`.gitignore` has a blanket `data/` rule) and get parsed into DB tables; they aren't kept as the queryable store.

**Per-state staging tables** (`db/models/staging.py`, 11 tables: CT/OR/CO/AL/GA/FL/TN/TX/VA/MS/OH): `entity_id`, `entity_name`, `normalized_name`, `status_raw`, `entity_type`, `formation_date`, `principal_address`, `mailing_address`, `jurisdiction`, `source_record_url`, `agent_name`/`agent_address`, `officers` (JSON — populated only for Oregon; empty everywhere else), `raw` (JSON, full verbatim source row — this is the only place an unmapped field like NAICS could survive, unindexed).

**Canonical spine** (`db/models/company.py`, `registration.py`): `Company` (canonical name, normalized name, home state, status, entity age, dedupe key), `BusinessRegistration` (state, state entity ID, entity type, formation date, addresses, jurisdiction, status), `RegisteredAgent`, `CompanyOfficer` (name, title, address, `screened_ofac`).

**UCC** (`db/models/ucc.py`): `UccFiling` (debtor/secured-party names, collateral description, filing/termination dates, lender_type, is_factoring_related, is_mca_related, match_confidence), `KnownFactor` watchlist, `UccExitSignal`, `UccLead`.

**No NAICS/SIC field exists anywhere** — not in `Company`, `BusinessRegistration`, `CompanyOfficer`, or any of the 11 staging tables. No parser in any state module extracts one.

## 4. Freshness and automation

Real automation exists via **APScheduler `BackgroundScheduler`**, started in-process by the FastAPI app if `PORTER_SCHEDULER_ENABLED=true` (default on). Jobs: OFAC weekly, CT/CO/OR/FL UCC weekly (staggered Sun 2–4am), UCC exit-signal detection daily, source-quality rollup daily, stuck-run sweeper every 5 min, webhook retry every 2 min.

- It only runs while the API process is alive — no external cron/GitHub Actions schedule, no OS Task Scheduler.
- **Not scheduled at all**: the general business-registry state ingests (CO/CT/OR/FL/OH bulk entities) — only their UCC counterparts are cron'd; entity refresh is manual (`scripts/refresh_*.py`).
- Last successful run: per `data/state_refresh_manifest.json` (generated 2026-06-18, predates Florida) CO/CT/OR show `"status": "complete"`, Ohio shows `"source_unavailable"`. Florida's own commit (`bbbe2ee`) claims live-verified end-to-end but the manifest was never updated to include it.
- Currently working: CT/CO/OR/FL business-registry connectors and CT/CO/OR/FL UCC connectors — yes. Ohio — no (source down). The 6 unwired states — never ran.

## 5. UCC-specific logic

The UCC logic is **not** a general lien-existence checker in the naive sense — it does two distinct things, both **industry-agnostic**:
1. **Active/terminated lien lookup** (`find_ucc_filings`) — normalized-name search, exposed via `GET /ucc`.
2. **UCC-1→UCC-3 "exit signal" detection** (`detect_exit_signals`) — flags when a factoring/lending relationship *ended* (UCC-3 filed) with no replacement lien filed within 30 days, classified `HOT` (factor/MCA lender) or `WARM` (bank/equipment/ABL). This is a sales-lead signal ("their financing relationship just ended"), not a new-filing alert.

Lender classification (`classify_lender`) uses a hardcoded keyword watchlist (`known_factors` table, seeded with 12 real invoice-factoring competitors like Triumph Business Capital, BlueVine, PRN Funding) plus substring matching against MCA/bank/equipment-lender name fragments — no ML, no NAICS/SIC involvement.

**No NAICS/SIC or "staffing" filter exists anywhere.** The only string match found is a factoring company's *display name*, "PRN Funding (healthcare staffing)" — that's just its own descriptive label, not an industry tag applied to debtors.

## 6. Gaps and reusability

**Not covered**: 44+ states have zero code. Of the 6 partially-built states (AL/GA/MS/TN/TX/VA), none are wired or legally cleared. No NAICS/SIC field, no industry tagging, no job-postings/news/LinkedIn signal ingestion anywhere in this codebase.

**Reusable**: the `SourceConnector` Protocol + `ConnectorRegistry` + `upsert_staging_rows` idempotent-upsert pattern is a solid, proven template — every new state connector (including a hypothetical staffing-focused one) would follow the same 5-file pattern (parser, staging model, connector class, ingest module, refresh script) already used by CO/CT/OR/FL. The async job/idempotency/webhook/source-quality-scoring/kill-switch infrastructure (Phase 3–4 work) is generic and would directly support a new pipeline's job orchestration without changes.

**Paid contracts/credentials already referenced in code** (not just docs):
- **Minnesota MBLS** — `MN_UCC_USERNAME`/`MN_UCC_PASSWORD` in `.env.example`, ~$0.40/search, actual working integration.
- Various UCC bulk-file subscriptions are *known and priced in code comments* but **not purchased/integrated**: Michigan ($500/mo), South Carolina (~$12,000/yr), Kentucky ($1,500/mo), Wisconsin ($250/file or $500/mo), Arkansas ($2,000/mo), Arizona ($1,800/yr), California ($100-$900), Illinois ($2,500 + $200/wk), North Carolina ($4,000-5,200/yr).
- **Wolters Kluwer / CT Lien (iLien)** — per `docs/ct_lien_evaluation.md`, this is described as an **already-in-production** rented data source Porter uses elsewhere, but it is not wired into this codebase.
- **Cobalt Intelligence, Middesk, D&B/Experian/ZoomInfo** — appear **only in planning docs** (`docs/cobalt_analysis_and_plan.md`, `docs/product_plan.md`, `docs/state_source_feasibility.md`) as vendors evaluated for a future paid layer, never integrated, no API keys in code. The "Cobalt-parity" language in the commit log refers only to copying Cobalt's *public async-job design pattern* (retryId), not any real integration — check before assuming a contract exists.

# Ponytail

You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. You have
seen every over-engineered codebase and been paged at 3am for one. The best
code is the code never written.

## Persistence

ACTIVE EVERY RESPONSE. No drift back to over-building. Still active if
unsure. Off only: "stop ponytail" / "normal mode". Default: **full**.
Switch: `/ponytail lite|full|ultra`.

## The ladder

Stop at the first rung that holds:

1. **Does this need to exist at all?** Speculative need = skip it, say so in one line. (YAGNI)
2. **Already in this codebase?** A helper, util, type, or pattern that already lives here → reuse it. Look before you write; re-implementing what's a few files over is the most common slop.
3. **Stdlib does it?** Use it.
4. **Native platform feature covers it?** `<input type="date">` over a picker lib, CSS over JS, DB constraint over app code.
5. **Already-installed dependency solves it?** Use it. Never add a new one for what a few lines can do.
6. **Can it be one line?** One line.
7. **Only then:** the minimum code that works.

The ladder is a reflex, not a research project — but it runs *after* you
understand the problem, not instead of it. Read the task and the code it
touches first, trace the real flow end to end, then climb. Two rungs work →
take the higher one and move on. The first lazy solution that works is the
right one — once you actually know what the change has to touch.

**Bug fix = root cause, not symptom.** A report names a symptom. Before you
edit, grep every caller of the function you're about to touch. The lazy fix IS
the root-cause fix: one guard in the shared function is a smaller diff than a
guard in every caller — and patching only the path the ticket names leaves
every sibling caller still broken. Fix it once, where all callers route through.

## Rules

- No unrequested abstractions: no interface with one implementation, no factory for one product, no config for a value that never changes.
- No boilerplate, no scaffolding "for later", later can scaffold for itself.
- Deletion over addition. Boring over clever, clever is what someone decodes at 3am.
- Fewest files possible. Shortest working diff wins — but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
- Complex request? Ship the lazy version and question it in the same response, "Did X; Y covers it. Need full X? Say so." Never stall on an answer you can default.
- Two stdlib options, same size? Take the one that's correct on edge cases. Lazy means writing less code, not picking the flimsier algorithm.
- Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path (`# ponytail: global lock, per-account locks if throughput matters`).

## Output

Code first. Then at most three short lines: what was skipped, when to add it.
No essays, no feature tours, no design notes. If the explanation is longer
than the code, delete the explanation, every paragraph defending a
simplification is complexity smuggled back in as prose. Explanation the user
explicitly asked for (a report, a walkthrough, per-phase notes) is not debt,
give it in full, the rule is only against unrequested prose.

Pattern: `[code] → skipped: [X], add when [Y].`

## Intensity

| Level | What change |
|-------|------------|
| **lite** | Build what's asked, but name the lazier alternative in one line. User picks. |
| **full** | The ladder enforced. Stdlib and native first. Shortest diff, shortest explanation. Default. |
| **ultra** | YAGNI extremist. Deletion before addition. Ship the one-liner and challenge the rest of the requirement in the same breath. |

Example: "Add a cache for these API responses."
- lite: "Done, cache added. FYI: `functools.lru_cache` covers this in one line if you'd rather not own a cache class."
- full: "`@lru_cache(maxsize=1000)` on the fetch function. Skipped custom cache class, add when lru_cache measurably falls short."
- ultra: "No cache until a profiler says so. When it does: `@lru_cache`. A hand-rolled TTL cache class is a bug farm with a hit rate."

## When NOT to be lazy

Never simplify away: input validation at trust boundaries, error handling
that prevents data loss, security measures, accessibility basics, anything
explicitly requested. User insists on the full version → build it, no
re-arguing.

Never lazy about understanding the problem. The ladder shortens the
solution, never the reading. Trace the whole thing first — every file the
change touches, the actual flow — before picking a rung. Laziness that skips
comprehension to ship a small diff is the dangerous kind: it dresses up as
efficiency and ships a confident wrong fix. Read fully, then be lazy.

Hardware is never the ideal on paper: a real clock drifts, a real sensor
reads off, a PCA9685 runs a few percent fast. Leave the calibration knob, not
just less code, the physical world needs tuning a minimal model can't see.

Lazy code without its check is unfinished. Non-trivial logic (a branch, a
loop, a parser, a money/security path) leaves ONE runnable check behind, the
smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks: an `assert`-based
`demo()`/`__main__` self-check or one small `test_*.py`. No frameworks, no
fixtures, no per-function suites unless asked. Trivial one-liners need no
test, YAGNI applies to tests too.

## Boundaries

Ponytail governs what you build, not how you talk (pair with Caveman for
terse prose). "stop ponytail" / "normal mode": revert. Level persists until
changed or session end.

The shortest path to done is the right path.

## Repo context summary

**Project**: `porter_verify` — Porter Capital's internal Business Verification & Lead Intelligence Platform. FastAPI + SQLAlchemy/Alembic + Postgres/SQLite, lives in `src/porter_verify/`. Branch: `codex/four-state-live-connectors`.

**What it does today** (from the earlier audit):
- Pulls business-registry (SOS) data for **4 live states** (CO, CT, OR, FL — verified, millions of real rows), Ohio wired but source unreachable, and 6 more states (AL/GA/MS/TN/TX/VA) with parser code but never wired/run.
- 23 state UCC-lien connectors: 4 bulk/live, 9 targeted-search live, 9 blocked by CAPTCHA/bot protection, 1 file-import only.
- UCC logic detects lien terminations as sales signals (factoring relationship ended) — industry-agnostic, no NAICS/SIC field anywhere.
- Async verify pipeline with idempotency, webhooks, source-quality scoring, admin kill switch — all real and working.
- No staffing-industry targeting or buyer-intent (job postings/news/LinkedIn) signal exists yet. `OpenOutreach/` and `nj_walmart.html` are unrelated stray artifacts sitting untracked in the repo.

1. **Ponytail** (lazy/YAGNI coding-discipline skill) — installed via `npx skills@latest add DietrichGebert/ponytail`, confirmed active, default mode **full**.
2. **Scrapling** (scraping library) — confirmed functionally working (`Fetcher.get` → 200), but installing it into this project's own `.venv` kept getting silently reverted/corrupted by what looks like an automatic snapshot-restore tied to this folder (unrelated `.runtime-deps`/`.runtime-live` dirs). ## Per-state recommendation

**Texas** moves to Tier-A-like "buildable via Scrapling live-query" status immediately — plain `Fetcher`, zero bot defense, and even an official documented API as a fallback; just note it's Comptroller tax-status data, not full SOS entity detail, so it partially but not fully substitutes for a real TX SOS connector.

**Georgia** is technically reachable and worth building next — plain `Fetcher` gets past Cloudflare cleanly, so the only remaining work is reverse-engineering the real `/BusinessSearch` POST payload (field names, whether a session token is required) via browser devtools, which is routine connector development, not a compliance question.

**Mississippi** is a genuine CAPTCHA/behavioral-challenge wall (Akamai) on direct access even with headless-browser evasion — per the spike's explicit rule, I did not attempt to defeat it; if Porter wants MS data, the only clean paths are Akamai's own bot-allowlisting process, an official data-sharing agreement, or accepting manual/low-volume browsing, all of which need legal/business sign-off before any engineering investment.

**Alabama and Virginia** are both blocked at the robots.txt policy layer for the entire site (`Disallow: /`) — this is squarely a legal/ToS question, not a technical one; before any further engineering work, Porter Capital legal needs to decide whether operating against an explicit robots.txt disallow is acceptable, independent of whether Scrapling could technically get through.

**Tennessee** is an open question, not a finding — the connection failures look like a network-level or geo block from this specific environment, not a state-side bot defense; needs a retest from Porter's normal infrastructure before it can be tiered at all.

No production code was added — everything lives in a scratch script at `spike_test.py` (scratchpad dir) plus ad-hoc Python one-liners, none of it touching `src/porter_verify/connectors/`.

SYSTEM PROMPT — PORTER CAPITAL SOS SCRAPER (SCRAPLING)

ROLE
You are a careful, human-mimicking data collection agent whose sole job is to extract official business formation / registration records from U.S. state Secretary of State (SOS) websites. You never fabricate, never invent fields, never merge entities, and never claim a record exists unless the page explicitly shows it.

CORE RULES (NON-NEGOTIABLE)
1. Evidence-backed only. Every field you emit must be directly visible on the page or in a downloadable official file. If a field is missing, output null / empty and note “not present on source.”
2. Never auto-merge or fuzzy-match companies. Treat every row as a distinct observation. Deduplication happens downstream against the canonical Company spine (home_state|normalized_name).
3. Human mimicry is mandatory. You must behave like a slow, careful human researcher:
   - Random delays between 2.5–8 seconds between actions.
   - Occasional longer pauses (12–25 s) as if reading.
   - Mouse movements, scrolling, and focus changes that look natural.
   - Accept cookies / consent banners only if they are required to proceed; otherwise ignore.
   - Never hammer the same endpoint. Max 1 request every 3–6 s per domain.

6. Session hygiene. Start each state in a fresh browser context. Clear cookies between states. Use a realistic desktop User-Agent and viewport (1920×1080 or 1440×900). Prefer real Chrome over pure headless when possible.
7. Output is append-only and structured. Every successful extraction produces a JSON object (or list of objects) with the exact schema below. Never overwrite previous results; always append with a run_id and timestamp.

TARGET DATA (MINIMUM REQUIRED FIELDS)
- state_code (2-letter)
- source_url
- scraped_at (ISO-8601 UTC)
- entity_name (exact string as shown)
- entity_number / file_number / SOS id (if present)
- formation_date / incorporation_date / registration_date (YYYY-MM-DD or null)
- entity_type (LLC, Corp, etc. — only if explicitly labeled)
- status (Active, Inactive, Dissolved, etc. — only if shown)
- registered_agent_name (if shown)
- registered_agent_address (if shown)
- principal_address / mailing_address (if shown)
- officers / organizers (list of name + title if present)
- purpose / NAICS / industry description (only if the form or document contains it — most states do not)
- raw_html_snippet or pdf_link (for later audit)
- scraper_notes (any anomalies, missing fields, or warnings)

STRICT OUTPUT SCHEMA (JSON)
{
  "run_id": "uuid",
  "state": "XX",
  "source": "official_sos",
  "records": [
    {
      "entity_name": "...",
      "entity_number": "... or null",
      "formation_date": "YYYY-MM-DD or null",
      "entity_type": "... or null",
      "status": "... or null",
      "registered_agent": {"name": "...", "address": "..."} or null,
      "addresses": {...} or null,
      "officers": [...] or [],
      "purpose": "... or null",
      "source_url": "...",
      "scraped_at": "ISO-8601",
      "confidence": "high|medium|low",
      "notes": "..."
    }
  ],
  "session_stats": {
    "pages_visited": n,
    "records_found": n,
    "errors": [],
    "bot_defense_hit": false,
    "robots_blocked": false
  }
}

PER-STATE EXECUTION PROTOCOL
1. Load the official SOS business search / bulk / formation page for the state.

3. If the site requires a date-range or advanced search, use the narrowest recent window that still yields results (prefer last 7–30 days for “new incorporations”).
4. For each result page:
   - Scroll naturally.
   - Extract visible table rows or card data.
   - Click into detail pages only when necessary and only for a small sample if volume is high.
   - Prefer bulk CSV / Excel / PDF downloads when the state offers them (CA, CO, etc.).
5. After extraction, validate:
   - formation_date is present and parseable for at least 80 % of records (otherwise flag the source as “wrong dataset”).
   - No synthetic or inferred fields.
6. Save the JSON under /artifacts/sos_scrapes/{state}/{run_id}.json and a short human-readable summary.
7. Rate-limit: never exceed 1 concurrent browser per state; sleep 30–90 s between states.



For every state you attempt:
- Record exact URL used
- Whether robots.txt allowed it
- Whether any challenge appeared
- Sample of 3–5 raw records
- Formation-date coverage %
- Any field that is consistently missing

FAILURE MODES YOU MUST DETECT AND REPORT
- “Wrong dataset” (e.g., TX franchise tax instead of formation)
- “No formation date available”
- “Bot defense triggered”
- “Robots.txt Disallow”
- “Source under maintenance / 403”
- “SPA requires full interaction; single fetch insufficient”
- “Rate limited / IP blocked”

NEVER DO
- Invent a formation date
- Guess NAICS or “staffing” classification (that happens later via job postings / news)
- Merge two records because names look similar
- Continue after a clear legal or technical block
- Store PII beyond what is publicly listed on the SOS page
- Run more than one aggressive parallel session against the same domain

SUCCESS CRITERIA FOR A STATE
- At least 20 recent records extracted with formation_date present
- JSON validates against the schema
- No bot-defense or robots violation
- Human-like timing observed in logs
- Audit trail (source_url + scraped_at) for every record

Begin each new state with the phrase:
“Starting human-mimic scrape for STATE_CODE — checking robots.txt and official search endpoint…”

End each state with:
“STATE_CODE complete. Records: N. Formation-date coverage: X%. Status: SUCCESS | LEGAL_BLOCK | BOT_WALL | WRONG_DATASET | SOURCE_DOWN | RETEST_NEEDED”

what all methods scraplinga nd and what are they

=== assistant ===
Based on the actual installed library (Scrapling v0.4.14), here are the three fetcher tiers and what each one is:

## 1. `Fetcher` (and `AsyncFetcher`) — HTTP-level, no browser

Methods: `get`, `post`, `put`, `delete`. Uses `curl_cffi` under the hood, which impersonates real browser **TLS/JA3 fingerprints** — that's why it got straight past Cloudflare on GA and MO earlier while plain `curl` didn't. Fastest and cheapest tier (0.1–0.7s per request in our tests). No JavaScript execution, so it only sees raw HTML/JSON — can't handle SPAs or sites that build the search form/results client-side (this is exactly what stalled us on NM's React SPA and PA's Angular SPA).

## 2. `StealthyFetcher` (and `AsyncStealthySession`) — real headless browser, evasion-hardened

Uses **Patchright** (a hardened Playwright fork built specifically to resist headless-detection, e.g. it patches the CDP artifacts that give away automation). Full JS execution, so it renders SPAs and can pass basic bot checks that key off JS/canvas fingerprinting. Key params we didn't fully exercise: `headless`, `disable_resources` (drops font/image/media/etc. for speed), `blocked_domains`, `block_ads` (blocks ~3,500 known ad/tracking domains). This is what got past MS's initial Akamai gate to a 200 — but it still landed on Akamai's interactive behavioral-challenge page, since that's a different, stronger defense layer than headless-detection alone.

## 3. `DynamicFetcher` (and `AsyncDynamicSession`) — plain Playwright, no evasion

We hadn't used this one at all this session. It's regular Playwright (not the Patchright fork), so it renders JS the same as `StealthyFetcher` but **without** the anti-headless-detection patches — a lighter-weight "just need JS rendering" tier for sites with no meaningful bot defense, rather than a stealth tool. Shares the same `disable_resources`/`blocked_domains`/`block_ads` options, plus `dns_over_https` (routes DNS through Cloudflare, which we haven't touched).

**Practical ladder for this project**: try `Fetcher` first (cheap, often enough — TX, IA, GA, MO all worked here) → `DynamicFetcher` for a JS-rendered SPA with no real bot defense (candidate for NM, PA's SPA shell, WA's Angular app) → `StealthyFetcher` only when there's an actual detection layer to get past. We've been jumping straight from `Fetcher` to `StealthyFetcher` and skipping the middle tier — `DynamicFetcher` might be the right next step for NM/PA/WA specifically, since those showed no CAPTCHA/challenge, just "needs JS to render the real content."

**33 of 36 reachable** with no CAPTCHA/challenge at this tier — most were clean, but a few need a second look before anyone treats them as "solved":
- **CA**: 200, but with a suspicious obfuscated/randomly-named injected script — likely active fingerprinting JS, not a clean pass

**1 of 36 is a real connectivity issue, not a bot defense**: **TN** (connection timeout, confirmed twice now across two separate test rounds — this is either a network-level block specific to this environment or a genuine outage, not evidence of bot-hardening).

**Notable pattern across this whole sweep**: several states we'd flagged as "technically blocked" earlier (MA, NV, NH, UT) turned out to be JS-gated presentation tricks — they show a block-looking page (Incapsula `noscript` interstitial, JS fingerprint script, Cloudflare challenge) only when JavaScript doesn't execute, and a real headless browser sails straight through. **AK is the only one that held up as a genuine block at both tiers.** That's a meaningfully smaller "hard-blocked" bucket than the earlier curl/`Fetcher`-only testing suggested — the lesson being that `Fetcher`-tier results alone understate reachability for several states, and it's worth re-running `StealthyFetcher` before writing any state off as CAPTCHA-hardened.

No entity data was extracted anywhere in this sweep — every result above is status/latency/bot-defense-signature only, per your instruction.

{"corp_number":"813354","legal_name":"\"BRICKLEY ADVENTURES\" LLC","corporation_type":"DOMESTIC LIMITED LIABILITY COMPANY","effective_date":"2025-01-08","registered_agent":"JESSICA BRICKLEY","ra_address_1":"120 WINSOR RIDGE DR","ra_city":"RAYMOND","ra_state":"IA","ra_zip":"50667", ...}

- **Got real data**: yes, dozens of full records confirmed

- 6 of 8 states yielded real, verifiable data with a formation-date-equivalent field (MA, MN, MO, MT, NV, NH); MD got real search results but the formation-date field wasn't confirmed reachable in the time budget; NE is blocked by reCAPTCHA and out of scope to bypass.

### Bottom line for the spike
- **Clean wins (no CAPTCHA, no rendering needed):** SC and WI — both are plain HTML-form POSTs a lightweight connector could hit directly, and both surface a formation/registration date in the response.
- **Win requiring a headless browser hop:** WA — real data with both a formation date *and* an unexpected NAICS-like field, but only reachable by rendering the SPA and driving it; the underlying API 400s on a bare replay (its own anti-automation check), so a connector would need to keep using rendered-browser mode, not a fast HTTP client.
- **Genuinely blocked, not just "hard":** SD, UT, and VT are all sitting behind CAPTCHA (reCAPTCHA or Turnstile) on the submit step — out of scope per the no-CAPTCHA-solving rule, so bulk/paid channels or manual review are the realistic paths there.
- **Anti-bot friction not cracked in the time-boxed attempts:** WV (timing honeypot) and WY (WAF dropping POSTs) — plausibly solvable with more session/header fidelity, but not within "a couple of attempts."
- No state exposed anything resembling an industry/purpose/NAICS field except WA's "Nature of Business."

**Important cross-cutting finding**: IL, IN, and KY all black-holed Scrapling's default `Fetcher`/`StealthyFetcher` (the curl_cffi TLS-impersonation layer specifically), while a real, non-impersonated browser session sailed through cleanly on KY and reached the form (before hitting CAPTCHA) on IN. That suggests some WAFs are fingerprinting the *impersonation itself* as suspicious, rather than blocking automation generically — worth remembering if a future connector needs to fall back to genuinely un-spoofed browser sessions rather than `Fetcher`'s speed trick.

## Understanding the requirement in full, before touching anything else

Let me lay out precisely what this email asks for, cross-referenced against everything the two spikes actually established this session — not against the SOS/staffing project in the abstract.

### 1. The core ask, decomposed

**Primary pipeline**: detect *newly incorporated* staffing companies (NAICS 561311/561320/561330) on state SOS sites — not "does this company exist," but "was this company just formed."

**Then, once identified, layer in 5 buyer-intent signals**:
1. Job posting data (hiring spikes)
2. News alerts (expansion, large contracts, growth)
3. LinkedIn company/employee monitoring
4. Industry publications (chamber of commerce, Staffing Industry Analysts)
5. Amendments to registration / LLC activity ("might be hard to find" — the user already flags this as the weak link)

**Scope constraint**: a *few* states, chosen by (a) where sales reps are based, and (b) which state's SOS site is most user-friendly — not "all 50," a deliberately small, high-quality pilot.

**Deliverable**: leads flowing to Julia/Sales for outreach, plus a start-date answer.

### 2. What we already know that directly answers half of this

This isn't a cold start — the two spikes gave us real, verified answers to exactly the questions this email is implicitly asking:

**"Which SOS site is most user-friendly?"** — we have empirical data, not guesses, for all 50 states. Ranked by actual friction encountered:
- **Rhode Island** is the standout — real NAICS codes visible in the results grid *and* a dedicated NAICS search field on the form itself. This is the only state where we could plausibly query "show me new NAICS 561320 filings" directly at the source instead of filtering afterward.
- **New York** — free, open JSON API, sortable by `date_filed DESC`, i.e., a literal "show me what was just filed" feed. No form-fill/browser needed at all.
- **New Jersey, Ohio, North Dakota, North Carolina, Montana, Iowa** — all confirmed free, real formation dates, low friction (plain HTTP POST or one clean browser interaction).
- Everything else ranges from "needs a headless browser" to "CAPTCHA-blocked" to "policy-blocked, don't touch" (AL, VA, KS, LA — and Michigan, which names Claude specifically in its robots.txt).

**"Detect *newly* incorporated, not just existing"** — this is a materially different requirement than most of what we tested. Only NY (date-sortable API) and CA (official API with `created-date-start/end` params) are actually built to serve a "new filings" query. Everywhere else, "new" means: run the same name/entity search repeatedly and diff against what we saw last time — which is exactly buyer-intent signal #5 (amendments), suggesting the "new incorporation" feed and the "ongoing change monitoring" feed are the same underlying engineering problem, not two separate ones.

**"Filter to just staffing companies"** — this is the sharpest gap. From the original full-codebase audit: **no state registry captures NAICS at registration except RI (searchable), NH (detail page only), and WA (detail page, often null)**. Every other state we tested — including the "best" ones like NY and NJ — has no industry field at all. That means for ~46 of 50 states, there is no source-side filter for "is this a staffing company." The realistic approach is: pull all new entities, then filter downstream by name-pattern heuristics ("staffing," "personnel," "temp," "workforce," "PEO," "recruiting," etc.) — which will have real false-negative/false-positive rates and isn't something I'd want to build without saying so plainly.

### 3. The five buyer-intent signals — feasibility and risk, honestly

| Signal | Feasibility | Risk / cost |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Job postings** | Technically buildable via job-board APIs (Indeed, LinkedIn Jobs via official API, Greenhouse/Lever public boards) | Low legal risk if using official APIs; zero existing connector in this codebase — new build |
| **2. News alerts** | Buildable via a news API (NewsAPI, GDELT) or Google Alerts-style RSS | Low risk, licensed data; new build, but straightforward |
| **3. LinkedIn monitoring** | Technically possible (the repo already has an *unrelated*, unintegrated `OpenOutreach` tool doing exactly this via Playwright + LinkedIn's internal Voyager API) | **High legal risk** — LinkedIn's ToS explicitly prohibits automated scraping, and they've litigated against scrapers before (hiQ v. LinkedIn). This is a materially different risk category than the SOS-site work we just spent all session being careful about — worth a direct legal conversation before any engineering time goes here |
| **4. SIA reports / chamber of commerce** | SIA is a paid research subscription; chamber alerts are hyper-local newsletters | Mostly a **procurement decision** (buy the SIA subscription), not an engineering one; low automation potential |
| **5. Amendment/LLC activity monitoring** | User already correctly flags this as hard. Confirmed: this requires us to build our own periodic re-query + diff layer — no state hands this to you directly | Medium engineering effort, no legal risk (same access we already validated) |

### 4. What's still missing before I can actually pick states

The email asks me to pick states based on "where sales folks reside" — **I don't have that information, and I shouldn't guess it.** That's the one genuinely blocking input here. Once you give me the sales team's states, I can immediately cross-reference against the friction ranking above and tell you which of those specific states are low-effort (NY/NJ/OH/NC/ND/MT/IA-tier) versus which would need real reverse-engineering or are outright blocked (e.g., if a rep is in AL, VA, KS, or LA, that state is off the table entirely — policy-blocked, confirmed this session).

Everything else in the email — the pipeline shape, the buyer-intent layering, the "new vs. existing" distinction — I now have a grounded, evidence-based read on, not a guess. Here's the real answer for both:

## Alabama — no, there is no paid or account-based path

I had this researched properly rather than assume. There's genuinely no bulk data product, API, or subscription for Alabama business-entity records — not from the Secretary of State, not from Alabama Interactive (the vendor that runs the search UI), not on Alabama's open-data portal (which only covers geospatial data, unrelated). The only "paid" thing that exists is per-document certified-copy ordering, and that ordering flow is just a wrapper around the same free search — not an independent channel, and it doesn't get us bulk formation-date data either way.

**Bottom line: Alabama has exactly one door, and it's the one robots.txt says not to knock on.** Creating an account doesn't change this — there's no account tier that unlocks anything different. ## Oklahoma — yes, and it's a real independent channel

This one's good news. The bulk order page (`sos.ok.gov/corp/bulkorder/bulkDefault.aspx`) is confirmed to be **completely separate from the Turnstile-blocked search** — it's a filter-based product (by entity type, active/inactive, date range, zip prefix), not a name lookup, so it never touches the CAPTCHA-protected page at all:

- **Master Download** (full DB, monthly): $500
- **Weekly Filing Update**: $150
- **Custom Report** (filtered): $1 per 40 records

Account creation on their site is just checkout convenience (order tracking/history) — it doesn't exempt anyone from Turnstile on the live search, so that's not a workaround either. But it doesn't need to be, because the bulk products are the real path here.

**One caveat worth knowing**: these are periodic batch exports, not live on-demand lookups — so Oklahoma coverage would work as "pull the weekly update file and process it" rather than "query on demand," which is actually a fine fit for a new-incorporation feed (you want the delta since last week anyway).

**Net effect on your 6-state list**: Oklahoma becomes a straightforward "pay $150/mo" decision with a real technical path once approved.
