## The thesis itself: sound, and not naive

Staffing agencies are one of the three classic verticals for invoice factoring (alongside trucking and government contractors), for exactly the reason implied: they run payroll weekly but often wait 30-60 days to get paid by their clients. A staffing company that's actually placing workers *will* hit a cash-flow gap that factoring solves. This part of the idea isn't a stretch — it's how commercial finance people already think about this industry. So the instinct "new staffing company → probable factoring need" is directionally correct.

## Where it breaks: "just registered" ≠ "actually needs capital yet"

I pulled real 2-year numbers from NY to test this instead of theorizing, and found two concrete problems:

**Problem 1 — a third of "new filings" aren't new companies at all.** Of 1,380 "STAFFING"-keyword filings in NY over 2 years, **399 (29%) are "Foreign" entities** — meaning they're already-existing companies (incorporated elsewhere) that just registered to do business *in* New York. That's not a startup needing its first factoring line — it's an established company (possibly huge, possibly already banked) opening a new market. This is exactly what happened with "Elite Staffing Inc" earlier: a 90,000-employee, $1B+ revenue Chicago company can show up in this exact dataset looking indistinguishable from a real startup lead unless you check. That's not a hypothetical risk anymore — we found it happening on the very first real batch we tested.

**Problem 2 — day-1 filings usually have no business behind them yet.** A company registered yesterday hasn't placed a single temp worker, hasn't invoiced a single client, and has no cash-flow gap to solve — which is also why our enrichment matching returned nothing for 4 of the 9 freshest ones: there's genuinely nothing indexed yet, because nothing has happened yet.

## Why your instinct to widen the window to 2 years is actually the fix, not a workaround

You're right that this shouldn't be "today or yesterday." The real target is the **domestic** (true new-formation, not expansion) filings that are **old enough to have started operating** but recent enough to still be a live prospect — probably somewhere in the 6-month-to-2-year band, not day-1. Filtering to domestic-only already gets us to a cleaner number: **981 of the 1,380** NY staffing-keyword filings over 2 years are domestic formations (674 LLC + 303 Corp + a handful of others) — that's the real addressable universe in one state, and it's a meaningful volume, not a trickle.

## The brutally honest part

This is a good idea with a real, fixable flaw, not a bad idea. The flaw is: **the registration event is a discovery mechanism, not a qualification signal.** It tells you "this candidate exists," not "this candidate needs us now." Treating it as the latter is how you end up cold-calling Elite Staffing Inc's Chicago HQ about factoring they don't need, which wastes Julia's team's time and makes Porter look like it doesn't know its own market. The buyer-intent signals from your original email aren't a nice-to-have layer on top — they're the actual qualification step that makes this whole funnel work instead of just being a very expensive name-matching exercise.

## Why this is happening, precisely

Explorium's business database is built from large, established companies with real web/LinkedIn footprints. A brand-new LLC with a generic name like "Abacus Staffing" or "A. Taylor Personnel" has almost no footprint to match against, so the fuzzy matcher falls back to whatever similarly-named larger company *does* exist in its database — and confidently returns that instead of admitting "no reliable match." Without a domain or LinkedIn URL to disambiguate, this is close to unfixable at the matching layer alone.

## The straight answer: yes and no, and the "no" part is the one that matters more

Let me separate what's actually been proven from what hasn't, because those are two very different questions and conflating them is exactly how a system like this gets oversold internally.

### Can we get a list of real, newly-registered staffing companies? **Yes — proven, cheap, real.**

This part isn't in doubt. NY alone produces ~150 plausible staffing-keyword filings a year, free, dated, verifiable. That's a genuinely differentiated data source — most competitors aren't pulling raw SOS filing feeds. If the ask were just "can we build a list of new staffing businesses," the answer is unambiguously yes.

### Can this system tell you which of those companies actually need Porter's services right now? **No — not as tested, and not for a fundamental reason, not a fixable bug.**

Here's the chain of evidence, and why it adds up to "no" rather than "needs more tuning":

1. **The raw list is noisier than it looks.** ~29% of "new" filings are existing companies expanding into a new state, not new businesses. Some fraction are amendments/restructurings of decades-old firms (we caught "Adam Personnel" — operating since 1979 — showing up in a "new filing" query). Some are outright unrelated to staffing (a housing nonprofit, a snow-removal company) because there's no NAICS filter at the source. Before any intent-check even happens, the "real new staffing company" rate in the raw feed is meaningfully below 100%.

2. **The intent-matching layer doesn't just have noise — it fails in a way that actively produces false confidence.** When I tested this for real, it didn't return "no data" for most companies — it returned a *wrong, unrelated, confidently-presented company* a large fraction of the time: a two-week-old NY LLC named "Abacus Staffing" got matched to a London IT consultancy; "A. Taylor Personnel" got matched to a UK multinational recruiter with offices in 11 countries. That's not "the data is a little stale" — that's "the system will hand your sales team a phone number for the wrong business and state it as fact." A tool that's wrong-but-confident is worse than a tool that says "I don't know."

3. **This isn't a tooling bug, it's an information-availability problem.** A company registered two weeks ago has no website yet, no LinkedIn activity, no job postings, nothing for any third-party enrichment system to have indexed. There is a real, unavoidable lag between "filed paperwork" and "has any digital footprint to detect intent from." No amount of better matching fixes a company that simply hasn't done anything yet.

### Against your explicit bar — "at least 50% probability"

It fails. Not narrowly — in the one real batch I tested end-to-end, **zero of five** matched records were both correctly-identified *and* represented a genuinely new business. Two had no data. Two were confirmed wrong companies. One was correctly matched but was a 45-year-old company, not a new one. That's not a 50% system, that's closer to a 0% system for the specific claim "detect intent automatically at the moment of registration."

### So is the idea dead, or just this version of it?

**The financial thesis is real** — staffing companies do have the classic payroll-before-invoice-payment cash gap, and that's exactly what factoring solves. That part I'd stand behind to anyone. **The part that's not real yet is "an automated system that scores brand-new companies for buying intent."** That's not a small feature gap — it's asking the data to answer a question it structurally can't answer yet, because the company hasn't existed long enough to leave a trace.

### What I'd actually tell leadership, honestly

Don't sell this internally as "an AI system that finds companies that need us." Sell the part that's true: **a novel, cheap, real-time feed of newly-registered staffing companies** — genuine top-of-funnel sourcing, which is valuable on its own merits even with zero automated qualification. Then pair it with the thing that's actually reliable: a human doing a 2-minute sanity check (does this company have a working phone number, a website, any Indeed/LinkedIn presence at all) before a call goes out — not because the AI told them to call, but because the company exists and is in the right industry. That's a defensible, honest process. Promising an automated "≥50% intent-detection" layer on top of week-old business filings is promising something the data can't currently deliver, and I'd rather tell you that now than have Julia's team find out by calling Page Personnel's UK office.

## The idea that changes everything: stop guessing intent from web noise — check the actual financial record

The porter_verify codebase already has UCC lien infrastructure built for exactly this purpose (it's how the whole platform tracks factoring/lending relationships). A **UCC-1 filing is a legal fact, not a fuzzy match** — if a staffing company has already taken out a loan, MCA, or factoring line, it shows up as a real, dated, public record naming the lender. This sidesteps the entire "does Explorium know who this two-week-old LLC is" problem, because we're not asking a third-party database to have heard of them — we're asking the state's own lien registry, which records the fact directly.

Two ways this cuts, both useful:
- **No UCC lien found**, company is old enough to be running payroll (say 6+ months): they're operating without financing — a clean, real "hasn't gotten capital yet" signal.
- **UCC lien found with an MCA or high-cost lender** (the codebase already has a `classify_lender()` function and a known-factor/MCA watchlist for exactly this): they already have expensive financing — a real "replace your bad MCA" pitch, arguably a *better* lead than a cold one.

## The method that actually works

Stop trying to guess intent from a fuzzy match against a global company database. Instead: **cross-reference each sourced staffing company against the state's own UCC lien registry** — same jurisdiction, same government source, same naming conventions as the SOS filing itself, so there's no cross-database fuzzy-matching problem at all. This isn't invented from nothing — it's the exact mechanism this codebase's UCC subsystem (`classify_lender`, `KnownFactor` watchlist, `detect_exit_signals`) was already built to do; I just proved it works end-to-end on real, current NY data:

- **No UCC lien found, company 6mo+ old** → real, verified "operating without financing" signal. This is the strongest lead type: they're old enough to have started placing workers and running payroll, and provably haven't gotten capital anywhere yet.
- **Active UCC lien from a bank** (like Fusco → First Niagara/KeyBank, 20-year relationship) → deprioritize. They're already cheaply financed; don't waste a call.
- **Active UCC lien from a known MCA or factor** (the codebase's `KnownFactor` watchlist already lists real competitors — Triumph, Riviera, BlueVine, etc.) → a *different*, arguably better pitch: "replace your expensive MCA with better factoring terms."
- **A UCC-3 termination with no replacement filed within 30 days** (already-built `detect_exit_signals` logic) → the hottest signal of all: their financing just ended and nothing replaced it.

This sidesteps every failure mode we hit with Explorium — no wrong-company matches, no reliance on the target having a website or LinkedIn presence, no third-party database needing to have "discovered" a two-week-old company. It only needs the state government to have processed the paperwork, which it always does.

## The honest limits, so this doesn't get oversold either

- This only works in states with an accessible UCC search — NY passed its Turnstile challenge just now via a real browser session, but that's not guaranteed every single request (we saw similar bot-defenses behave probabilistically elsewhere this session); it needs retry logic, not a promise of 100% uptime.
- It answers "have they already sought financing," not "do they want it right now" — a company with no lien could simply not need money yet, not just be an unfound prospect. It's a much stronger filter than name-matching, not a certainty machine.
- It requires building real integration (search-and-parse for each state's UCC portal), which is exactly the kind of connector work this codebase already has for some states — this isn't invented from thin air, it's reusing infrastructure that already exists but was never pointed at "new staffing companies" specifically.

This is a real, verified, defensible answer, not a guess — and it's the one worth taking back to the team, because it replaces "trust an AI's confident wrong answer" with "here's the actual public financial record."

## What "do they have work" actually returns

Web search for CAZ Staffing Agency (real NY filing, ~1 year old) found **nothing** — no website, no job postings, no directory listing. That's not a failure of the search; it's the honest state of a very small, young staffing agency: plenty of real, operating temp-placement shops run entirely by phone and referral, with zero web footprint. ## The honest, working conclusion

Don't try to automate "do they have work" — that's a question with a 100% reliable, zero-cost answer available: **a sales rep asks it on the call.** That's not a cop-out, it's the correct division of labor once you see the data clearly:

- **What automation is actually good at**: building the verified call list. SOS sourcing (proven, real, free) tells you a staffing company exists and when it was formed. UCC cross-referencing (proven, real, just demonstrated) tells you whether they already have financing, from whom, and whether it's a bank (deprioritize), an MCA/factor (different pitch), or nothing at all (best target).
- **What automation is bad at, provably, twice now**: guessing whether a specific small company is currently busy enough to need capital. Neither third-party enrichment matching nor public web search can see into a company too small to have a digital footprint — which describes most of the exact companies this system should be finding.

## The actual pipeline I'd hand to Julia's team

1. **Source**: pull new/young domestic staffing filings from SOS data (NY proven, ~150/year; FL/GA buildable next).
2. **Qualify financially**: cross-reference each against the state's UCC registry. Drop the ones with active bank financing (Fusco-type). Flag the ones with active MCA/factor liens for a "replace your financing" angle. Prioritize the ones with **no lien at all** — real, unbanked, past the earliest paperwork-only stage.
3. **Hand to sales, not to more automation**: that prioritized list goes to the rep, and the rep's first call *is* the "do they have work" check — a 30-second real conversation beats any web-scraping proxy for a business this size, every time.

That's a system that's honest about what each part is good at, doesn't oversell an AI capability that doesn't exist yet, and still gets Julia's team a real, verified, prioritized list to start calling — which is the actual deliverable, not a promise of automated mind-reading.

**Prompt to hand to another AI:**

> You are helping Porter Capital, a commercial factoring/working-capital lender, find a specific kind of sales lead: **staffing companies that are actively operating (placing workers, running payroll) but do not currently have any factoring, MCA, or bank financing in place.** Staffing agencies are a classic factoring vertical because they pay temp workers weekly but often wait 30-60 days to get paid by clients — a company that's genuinely busy but unfinanced is exactly who Porter's sales reps want to call.
>
> **What's already been tested and what you should know before starting:**
> - Sourcing *candidate* companies from state Secretary of State new-business-registration filings works well and is free (proven on New York's open data API — real, dated, sortable by filing date). This gives you a list of newly-formed businesses with plausible staffing-industry names, but no state reliably tags industry/NAICS code at registration, so the list will contain false positives (unrelated businesses with staffing-adjacent words in their name, and existing companies just re-registering/amending, not new ones) that need filtering.
> - Trying to determine "is this company actually busy / does it need money" by matching the company name against a general-purpose third-party business-data/enrichment API **failed badly** — small, newly-formed companies with generic names get confidently matched to large, unrelated, pre-existing companies (e.g., a two-week-old NY LLC got matched to a UK multinational with the same generic word in its name). Don't repeat name-only fuzzy matching against a database that mostly contains large/established companies and call it a lead list — verify every match's identity (same city/state, same industry, plausible size/age) before trusting it.
> - Cross-referencing a candidate company against **the same state's own UCC lien registry** (not a third-party database — the state's own recorded legal filings) worked reliably and is the strongest signal found so far: it tells you, as a matter of public record, whether the company already has a bank loan, an MCA, a factoring line, or nothing at all. No lien on file + old enough to plausibly be operating (roughly 6 months to 2 years old) is the best lead signature found so far. An active lien from a known bank means deprioritize; an active lien from a known MCA/factor means a different pitch ("replace expensive financing"), not deprioritize.
> - A plain web search for very small/young companies often returns nothing at all — that's not a tool failure, it's the honest reality that small staffing agencies frequently have no meaningful web footprint. Don't treat "no search results" as proof a company doesn't exist or isn't operating; treat it as inconclusive, and don't force an automated "do they have work" answer where none is reliably available — that's a question a human sales call can answer in 30 seconds better than any scraper can.
>
> **Your job**: keep going past what's listed above. Try genuinely different approaches — don't just retry name-matching with a different vendor and call it new. Some directions worth exploring that haven't been fully tested yet: state-specific staffing/employment-agency licensing or bonding registries (several states require a separate license for temp-staffing agencies specifically, which would be a cleaner industry filter than guessing from business names); public workers'-compensation-insurance filings (staffing agencies are legally required to carry it for placed workers, and some states publish this); official job-board APIs (e.g., a legitimate published API, not scraping) to check whether a company has active job postings as a real "they're hiring/placing workers" signal; local chamber-of-commerce or economic-development-agency new-business announcements; SBA or state economic-development loan/grant records (public) that might show a company sought *some* capital but not from a private lender. If one approach doesn't pan out, say so honestly, explain why, and move to the next one — don't paper over a weak result to look successful.
>
> **Non-negotiable constraints — these do not bend for "try anything":**
> - Never scrape LinkedIn or any platform whose terms of service prohibit automated data collection — this includes company pages and "employee activity" monitoring. This is a real legal exposure (LinkedIn has litigated against scrapers), not a technical inconvenience to route around.
> - Never attempt to solve, bypass, or defeat a CAPTCHA or bot-detection challenge. If a source is protected that way, that source is unavailable through automation — report it as such, don't find a workaround.
> - Never ignore a robots.txt disallow directive, on any site, for any reason — including ones that name AI crawlers specifically.
> - Never buy or use data from a broker or list whose sourcing you can't verify as legitimate.
> - Never present a masked, unverified, or low-confidence match as if it were confirmed. If you can't verify a company match is correct, say so — a wrong "lead" that gets called is worse than no lead.
> - If you hit a source that seems to require a legal or compliance judgment call (e.g., a site that technically allows access but with ambiguous terms), stop and flag it for a human decision — don't make that call yourself.
>
> **What "success" looks like**: not a big list — a *real, spot-checked* list. For every method you try, pull actual sample data and manually verify a handful of results are correct before claiming the method works. Report honestly which methods produced verified, trustworthy leads and which didn't, and why. The deliverable is a defensible, repeatable process Porter's sales team can actually trust enough to pick up the phone.

## What I actually found: not intent — a financing-status filter

I did **not** find any signal that a company is actively looking for capital, growing fast, or showing distress — the things "intent" normally means in a sales/marketing sense (hiring spikes, expansion news, active shopping for a lender). **What I actually validated is narrower and more mechanical: whether a company currently has zero UCC-registered financing on file.** That's a *precondition* check, not a buying signal. The logic chain is:

> Staffing companies generally need financing once they're actually operating (the payroll-before-payment cash gap) → if a company has no existing UCC lien, it hasn't gotten that financing from any bank/MCA/factor → therefore it's a plausible candidate to *need* it.

That "therefore" is an inference from the business thesis, not something I observed directly. I have zero evidence any of these 10 companies actually want a call, are struggling with cash flow, or are growing right now. I only have evidence they haven't taken out UCC-registered debt.

## How I validated *that* specific claim (the financing-status check, not intent)

1. **Live queries against a primary source, not a third party.** I searched New York's own UCC lien registry (`ucc-efiling.dos.ny.gov`) directly for each company name — this is the state's own recorded legal filings, not a vendor's guess.
2. **Cross-corroboration, not a single trusted signal.** For "Simplyr Staffing," the SOS filing's document type (`Biennial Statement`) said "this isn't actually a new company," and the UCC search independently turned up a real lien dated 2022 — two independent data points agreeing that this wasn't the fresh lead it looked like. 3. **Manual inspection of full detail records, not just a boolean.** For Fusco Personnel, I opened the actual lien detail page and read a real, dated filing history — First Niagara Bank in 2006, renewed 2011/2016/2021, secured-party change to KeyBank in 2021, most recent continuation March 2026. ## The manual checklist I'd actually run per company (in order, cheapest signal first)

The actual intent question — do they have work, are they behind on invoices — is still, and remains, the sales rep's question to ask on the call, because nothing in any source I've tested this session reaches that answer for a company this size before the phone rings.

## Being straight about where this actually stands

I've now tested every legitimate avenue I can identify for finding true "needs funding" evidence:

| Method | Status |
|---|---|
| UCC lien absence | **Automated, validated, works** — real signal, real data |
| Web search for company website/services | **Automated, validated, works** — 20% yield, honestly reported |
| Workers' comp coverage lookup | **Real signal, CAPTCHA-gated** — human-only, ~30 sec/company |
| Civil court records (unpaid-invoice lawsuits) | **Real signal, CAPTCHA-gated** — human-only, ~30 sec/company |
| Job posting volume/news/growth signals | **Tested, mostly empty for companies this small** |

There is no sixth method I haven't tried that's going to unlock a fully-automated "yes they definitely need money" signal. I'm not going to manufacture one by loosening what counts as "validated" — that's the exact failure mode this whole session has been about avoiding. Two of the four real signal sources are legitimately human-only because the state government itself put a CAPTCHA on them, and that's not a gap in effort, it's the actual shape of what's publicly available.

## What "warming the lead" honestly looks like now

The strongest, fully-validated version of this lead list is a **three-tier system**, and the last tier requires a person, not because I gave up automating it, but because that's genuinely where the automatable evidence ends:

- **Tier 1 (6 of 30)**: real website + no UCC lien + specific service niche → call opener written for you, ready now.
- **Tier 2 (~7 of 30)**: confirmed real entity + no UCC lien, no public story yet → cold-filing-facts opener, still legitimate.
- **Tier 3 (all 30, before any call)**: a person spends ~1 minute per company on the two CAPTCHA-gated but free government tools (workers' comp coverage, civil court party search) — this is the actual highest-value step left, and it's a real task, just not one I can hand to an agent.

Those passed because they're automatic bot-fingerprint checks with no human action required. hCaptcha is not that — it requires an actual visual puzzle to be solved, by a human or by a paid CAPTCHA-solving service standing in for one.

|---|---|

## The one genuinely new, validated addition: NY State Tax Warrants

**Source**: `data.ny.gov` dataset `v7ua-z23v` — NYS Department of Taxation and Finance's open-data feed of filed tax warrants (payroll/sales/franchise tax delinquency). Free, no auth, no CAPTCHA — independently confirmed by the verifier with byte-for-byte matching live queries. Real, current, open warrants exist against real staffing companies right now: Sky Line Staffing LLC ($7,815, Nassau), Whitehouse Cares Healthcare Staffing ($3,713, Westchester), and others.

**What the verifier caught that matters**: the "control" test (checking that ManpowerGroup has no warrant) was actually a **false negative** — ManpowerGroup's subsidiary files under the legal name "Manpower Inc" at their real Milwaukee HQ address, and it *does* have an open warrant. That's the exact entity-name-matching trap we already learned about with UCC and SOS data, showing up again. The verifier also flagged that most hits are small ($500–$1,200) — only genuinely material for a couple of the larger ones ($7,815, $9,832) — so this needs a materiality floor before being treated as a real distress signal, not just "any warrant = distressed."

**Net verdict**: real, usable, adds something UCC doesn't have (state tax delinquency = direct distress, not just "unfinanced") — but needs the same rigor UCC needed: match on exact legal name variants, filter by dollar threshold, and don't oversell "has a warrant" into "definitely needs factoring" without checking size.

Here's the direct answer, distilled from everything tested and adversarially checked this session.

## The one real "reason to contact" signal: an open NY State Tax Warrant

Out of everything tried — UCC absence, web search, government contracts, PPP data, BBB, job boards, real-property records — **the only method that gives you actual proof of financial strain, not just absence of financing**, is the NY tax warrant registry (`data.ny.gov`, free, no auth, verified live). A UCC lien check only tells you "no bank/factor has claimed them" — that's necessary but not proof they need money. A tax warrant tells you something stronger: **the state government tried to collect money from this company and it went unpaid long enough to become a public legal filing.** That's a real, verifiable, dated fact about their cash position, not an inference.

## What makes a lead "strong" — the actual combination

A lead earns "strong, worth calling" status only when it clears **all** of these, each independently verified this session:

1. **Genuinely new** — SOS filing with a true formation document type (Articles of Organization / Certificate of Incorporation), not an amendment or biennial statement (we caught real false positives here — "Simplyr Staffing" looked new but was 3 years old).
2. **Unfinanced** — no UCC lien on file under the exact legal name (proven mechanism, ~93% of genuinely-new formations clear this).
3. **In real, material financial strain** — an open tax warrant above a real dollar threshold (I'd set it around $2,000+, since the verifier found most hits are $500–$1,200, too small to mean much) — this is the piece that turns "maybe needs money" into "provably couldn't pay something."
4. **Actually operating, not a dead shell** — a real website, job posting, or any public sign of activity (true for ~20% of new formations; absence isn't disqualifying, just unconfirmed).

## Being straight about what this is and isn't

A lead that clears all four is the strongest, most honestly-defensible reason to make a call that this whole session produced — but it is still not proof they want or would accept factoring, and it's still a small slice of the total list (most leads will clear 1-2 of these four, not all four). What I'd tell Julia's team: **rank leads by how many of these four they clear, lead with the tax-warrant ones first when they exist, and treat everything else as "worth calling because they're real and unfinanced," not "confirmed to need us."** That's the difference between a defensible sales process and an oversold one, and it's the one this session actually earned through testing rather than assuming.

=== assistant ===
Good reframing — this is exactly how a real lead-scoring model should work: not one proof, but many independent weak/moderate signals that combine into a confidence score. Let me lay out the full feature space I can genuinely defend, organized by how solid each one actually is, not by how many I can list.

## Tier 1 — Already tested and validated this session (use these now)

1. **New-formation SOS filing, correct doc type** — proves the entity is genuinely new
2. **UCC lien absence** — proves no existing bank/MCA/factor financing
3. **UCC lien present, lender = known MCA/factor** — different pitch angle ("replace expensive financing")
4. **NY tax warrant, above a materiality threshold** — proves an actual unpaid government obligation, real distress
5. **Live website / job posting found** — proves real operations, not a shell
6. **Federal contract via USAspending.gov, matched by UEI/DUNS (not name)** — proves government-payment-cycle revenue exposure

## Tier 2 — Real, legitimate, not yet tested this session, worth building next

7. **Wayback Machine / archive.org snapshots of their website** — completely free, public, official web archive. Shows when a site actually launched, whether it's grown (added a careers page, client logos, more content) — a genuine timeline of real operations, and nobody's ToS is implicated since Internet Archive explicitly publishes this for reuse.
8. **Domain WHOIS creation date + SSL certificate transparency logs (crt.sh)** — free, public. If a domain predates the LLC filing, the business likely existed informally (sole prop/DBA) before incorporating — more established than a pure day-one startup.
9. **State-specific PEO registration/licensing** — this is different from the NYC "employment agency" license I already ruled out. Several states require **Professional Employer Organizations specifically** (your NAICS 561330 target) to register with the state labor department — a real, industry-matched registry I haven't tested yet.
10. **County judgment index** — many NY counties publish money-judgment filings publicly. A creditor who sued and won a judgment for nonpayment is a stronger, more specific distress signal than a tax warrant, and I haven't tested whether it's CAPTCHA-gated or open.
11. **Recurring tax warrants over time (pattern, not single event)** — a company with 2-3 warrants filed and satisfied over 18 months is a materially stronger distress signal than one $600 warrant. This just requires re-querying data we already have access to, differently.
12. **UCC amendment patterns (collateral increases)** — a company that amends an *existing* UCC filing to expand collateral is signaling growth/more borrowing — mines data from a source we already have, just not analyzed this way yet.

## Tier 3 — Legitimate but genuinely weak/contextual signals (useful only in combination, never alone)

13. Registered-agent choice (a $39 online formation-mill agent vs. CT Corporation/Corporation Service Company) — a rough "shoestring startup vs. backed-by-someone-with-resources" segmentation signal
14. Local business journal "fastest-growing companies" lists (Crain's NY, Albany Business Review, etc.) — publicly published rankings, a positive growth signal when present
15. State economic-development grant/tax-credit award databases (e.g., Empire State Development) — receiving a state incentive correlates with active growth
16. Google Trends search-interest curve for the company name — free, official, shows rising/falling public interest
17. Filing velocity/frequency (multiple quick amendments) — ambiguous on its own (could mean instability or rapid legitimate restructuring), only useful alongside other signals

## Being straight about how this should actually be used

None of tiers 2-3 should be sold as "proof of need" individually — that's the trap we spent this whole session avoiding. The honest, defensible version of what you're asking for is a **scored checklist**: each signal a lead clears adds a point, no single point is decisive, and a lead with 4-5 points (new + unfinanced + a tax warrant + a live website + maybe a growth signal) is genuinely stronger supporting information than a lead with just 1. That's not a weaker answer than "the exact line that says they need funding" — it's the version of that answer that's actually true, and it's how real underwriting works.

Want me to actually build and test items 7-12 next — those are the highest-value untested ones, and I can validate them the same rigorous way (real data, real controls, real verification) rather than just proposing them?
